- Target nodes are E and J.
- List E’s neighbors: F, L, M, B. Also an edge E–J? No, E connects to M and B and L and F only.
- Paths from E to J must go through at least one of M, B, or via F→…→J or L→M→J. Check junctions leading into J’s neighborhood. J’s neighbors: M, I, P, D.
- Enumerate disjoint route types:
  1) E–M–J
  2) E–L–M–J
  3) E–B–I–J
  4) E–B–C–I–J
  5) E–B–C–P–J
  6) E–B–N–O–P–J (or via C–O–P–J)
  7) E–F–G–H–I–J (or variants through B)
  8) E–F–G–H–B–…–J (then through I or P)
- To disconnect E from J, we can cut all access into J’s immediate neighborhood by removing edges that connect into {M, I, P, D}. D only connects to J and P, so cutting at J’s side is efficient.
- Observe minimal cut at the “entrances” to J:
  - Edge (M, J) blocks routes 1 and 2.
  - Edge (I, J) blocks routes through I (3 and 4).
  - Edge (P, J) blocks routes through P (5 and 6).
  - Edge (D, J) is not needed if P–J is cut, since E has no alternative to reach D except via P→D; but J–D being present would still allow …→P–D–J if (P, J) were cut. However P–D provides a bypass back to J, so to block P-routes we must ensure neither P–J nor D–J creates an entry. Cutting (P, J) alone is sufficient because any path via D must include edge (D, J). If we do not cut (D, J), E could go …→P–D–J. So we must cut either (P, J) or (D, J). Cutting (P, J) is enough only if we also cut (D, J) or cut P–D upstream. Since P–D is a separate edge, leaving (D, J) while cutting (P, J) still allows E→…→P→D→J. Hence we must also block D.
- Therefore a safe minimal cut is to remove three edges directly incident to J: (M, J), (I, J), and (D, J). This blocks entries via M, I, and D; remaining entry via P is neutralized because P cannot reach J without (P, J) or (D, J). Since (D, J) is cut, any P-based route cannot enter J.
- Check smaller sets:
  - Any two-edge cut cannot work: if we cut (M, J) and (I, J), path via …→P→J or …→P→D→J remains unless we also cut one of those edges. If we cut (M, J) and (P, J), paths via I remain. If we cut (I, J) and (P, J), path E–M–J remains. Thus at least three edges are needed.
- Verify after removing {(M, J), (I, J), (D, J)}:
  - All routes through M blocked.
  - All routes through I blocked.
  - All routes through P must try P–J or P–D–J; both end with a removed edge (P–J not removed, but D–J removed; however P–J remains so actually need to ensure it’s also removed). To avoid any remaining P→J direct edge, we should remove (P, J) instead of (D, J), or remove both. Minimal choice: remove (P, J) and either (I, J) or (M, J) etc.
  - Corrected minimal cut: remove (M, J), (I, J), (P, J). Then any path to J must use D, but D connects only to P and J; with (P, J) removed, the only way from D to J is (D, J), which still exists and would allow …→P–D–J unless we also ensure P can’t reach D? P–D edge exists, so E could go …→P–D–J. Hence we must cut (D, J) as well unless we cut P–D upstream.
- Minimal 3-edge cut can be: cut two of the three entrances (M, I, P/D pair counted as two edges if choose both J-edges there). To block the P/D entrance we must cut at least one of (P, D) or (D, J) or (P, J). But if cutting only one of the J-incident edges among P and D, the other may allow entry via the P–D link.
  - If we cut (P, J) and (D, J), the entire P/D entrance is blocked with two edges.
  - Then we still need to block M or I. So total edges = 3.
- Choose set: {(P, J), (D, J), (M, J)} for example. This blocks P/D and M; I-route still open via I–J unless also cut. So pick I instead of M: {(P, J), (D, J), (I, J)}. But M-route remains. Need four if we block both I and M. Wait, we need to block either M or I, not both, if P/D is blocked by two edges. However E can reach J via the remaining one (say M) only if that edge exists. So we must cut exactly one of {M–J, I–J} in addition to the two edges for P/D. Thus 3 edges suffice.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(P, J), (D, J), (M, J)] or [(P, J), (D, J), (I, J)]

  - Remove {I, C}: H still connected to G, and E–G–H exists. So connected.
Thus any pair fails.

Therefore, removing any two of {M, I, P} is sufficient to disconnect E from J:
- If M and I are removed, remaining potential route would be via P, but P has no remaining connection to E’s side except through I or M; without them, P connects to O and D only, not to E’s side. Thus no path.
- Similarly for pairs {M, P} or {I, P}.

Minimality:
- A single node removal cannot work:
  - Remove M: paths remain via I (E–H–I–J) or via P (E–H–I–P–J).
  - Remove I: paths remain via M (E–M–J) or via P (E–H–…–P–J).
  - Remove P: paths remain via M or I.
Thus minimum cut size is 2.

Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [M, I] (also valid: [M, P] or [I, P])

- Check if H can reach O without going through any of these four: impossible because O has no other neighbors.

- Single node removal? If remove C, path H–I–P–O remains. If remove P, path H–C–O or H–C–N–O or H–C–Y–O remains. If remove N, path H–C–O or H–I–P–O remains. If remove Y, path H–C–O or H–I–P–O remains. So no single node suffices.

- With P and N removed, O’s neighbors left: C and Y.
- Paths via C: H–C–O still exists unless C removed.

Thus minimum cut size is 3: for example {C, P, N} or {C, P, Y}.

- Two-node cuts fail because if they don’t include P, route via P exists; if they include P but not C, route via C exists; if include both C and P, path H–G–N–Y–O remains. So at least 3 nodes required.

- Direct short path: B–E–M uses E.
- All other routes funnel into the inner triangle and need F to reach M (via …–G–F–M or …–X–F–M).

- Removing only F is insufficient because B–E–M remains.
- Removing both E and F breaks all enumerated routes: from B via H/C/N cannot reach M because E and F are both absent; J/I/P/D/O components have no connection to M without F/E.

- Minimality: Any cut with fewer than 3 edges cannot disconnect E and H because H has degree 3; with only one or two removals, at least one incident edge to H remains, and E has routes to all three neighbors (B, G, I), yielding a path.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(G, H), (B, H), (I, H)]

1) Through H: H connects to E and B. From there, multiple connections lead to I (H–I directly as well). Since there is a direct edge H–I, any path via H reaches I unless H is removed or the edge’s alternate entries are cut elsewhere.
2) Through F: F connects to E and K and X and G. From F → E → … → I via E–M–J–I or E–H–I, etc.
3) Through K: K connects to F and X and G only. From K, the only way out to the rest is via F or X, then to E/H. So K-routes funnel into F or X anyway.
4) Through X: X connects to F and K and E and G. From X → E → … → I.

Therefore, every G→I path must pass through the small separator {H, E, F, X} in the left bridge area, and specifically, must use at least one of {H, E, F, X} that connects the left clique to the central region. But note:
- If we remove H alone, there remain paths G–F–E–…–I and G–X–E–…–I.
- If we remove E alone, there is still G–H–I (direct).
- If we remove F alone, paths via X–E and H exist.
- If we remove X alone, paths via F–E and H exist.

To disconnect, we need to block both independent channels:
A) The direct H–I channel (any path using H to reach I).
B) The E-mediated channel from the left cluster (via F or X to E to the center).

Minimum cut of size 2 works:
- Remove H to break the direct H–I and H-based routes.
- Remove E to break all routes via F/X/K toward the center.

Verify no remaining path:
- With H removed, G cannot go G→H→I or to B via H. Remaining neighbors are F, X, K.
- With E removed, nodes F/X/K cannot reach the central graph; F and X only connect among themselves and K, and to G. There is no edge from that left trio to I anymore.
Thus all G→I paths are blocked.

Check minimality:
- Any single node removal fails (as shown above).
- Size 2 is therefore minimal; {H, E} is a valid minimum cut. Other equivalent size-2 cuts do not work since replacing E by F or X leaves a path via the other to E; replacing H by something else leaves H–I path intact.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, E]
